This is a digital version of a book that was preserved for many generations on library shelves before being carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for its copyright to expire, allowing the book to enter the public domain. A "public domain" book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has ended. Whether a book is in the public domain can vary from one country to another. Public domain books serve as our gateways to the past, representing a vast wealth of history, culture, and knowledge that is often difficult to find otherwise.

Any marks, notations, or other marginal notes <note>original: "marginalia"</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file—a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and, finally, to you.

## Usage Guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public, and we act merely as their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive. Therefore, to continue providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent misuse by commercial parties. This includes placing technical restrictions on automated searches <note>original: "querying"</note>.

We also ask that you:

+ **Use these files for non-commercial purposes.** We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals; we request that you use these files only for personal, non-business reasons.

+ **Refrain from automated searches.** Do not send automated searches of any kind to Google’s system. If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition (OCR), or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to assist you.

+ **Maintain attribution.** The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ **Keep your use legal.** Whatever you do with these files, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that your actions are legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still under copyright varies from country to country, and we cannot offer guidance on whether any specific use of a particular book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. The legal liability for copyright infringement can be very severe.

## About Google Book Search

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world’s books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. # FOREWORD

Mind and Body—Mental States and Physical Conditions! To those who have satisfied themselves with merely the superficial aspects of things, these two things—mind and body, or mental states and physical conditions—seem to be as far apart as the two poles. They seem to be opposites and contradictions that are impossible to reconcile.

But to those who have looked beneath the surface of things, these two apparent opposites are seen to be so closely related and interconnected—so blended and mingled together in their manifestation <note>original: "manifestation"; how something shows or expresses itself in the physical world.</note>—that it is practically impossible to scientifically determine where one leaves off and the other begins. Their mutual action and reaction are so constant and close that it often becomes impossible to state positively *which* is the cause and which is the effect.

In the first place, Science now informs us that in all living substance, from a single cell to a mam- <note>The text ends mid-word; it likely continues as "mammal" on the following page.</note>

<summary>In this foreword, Atkinson introduces the inseparable relationship between the mind and the body, arguing that they are so deeply interconnected that it is difficult to distinguish cause from effect.</summary>
<keywords>mind-body connection, mental states, physical conditions, science, manifestation, William Walker Atkinson</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Foreword" of William Walker Atkinson’s *Mind and Body*. It concludes a sentence started on the previous page regarding the presence of mind in all living things, from a single cell to a mammoth.</meta>

->MIND AND BODY<-

-moth, there is and must be a Mind. There can be no Life without Mind. Mind, indeed, is held to be the very "living quality" <note>original: "livingness"</note> of Life—the greater the degree to which Mind is expressed, the higher the degree of Life. Moreover, the New Psychology <note>The "New Psychology" refers to an early 20th-century movement, closely related to "New Thought," which emphasized the power of the subconscious mind and mental suggestion on physical health.</note> informs us that all the processes of physical life depend upon the activities of the Subconscious Mind. It teaches that the Subconscious Mind is the essence of what was formerly called the Vital Force <term>Vital Force: A historical concept in biology and philosophy suggesting that living organisms are governed by a non-physical energy or "life-spark" distinct from purely chemical or physical forces.</term>—and is embodied in every cell, cell-group, or organ of the body.

Furthermore, this Subconscious Mind is susceptible <note>original: "amenable"</note> to suggestion, whether good or evil, from the conscious mind of its owner as well as from the outside world. When the subject of how Mental States influence Physical Conditions is studied, one sees that the Physical Condition is merely the reflection of the Mental State. This seems to solve the problem and the mystery of Health and Disease. 

But in this, as in everything else, there is an opposing perspective—the other side of the coin <note>original: "the other side of the shield"</note>. Let us look at the other side of the question: 

Just as we find that wherever there is living substance there is Mind, so do we find...

<summary>Atkinson explains that the "Subconscious Mind" is the source of physical life and is influenced by mental suggestions, which can determine a person's health or disease.</summary>
<keywords>Subconscious Mind, Vital Force, New Psychology, mental suggestion, mind-body connection, health and disease, physical conditions</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Foreword" from page 10, completing the thought that mind and matter are inseparable in living beings.</meta>

# FOREWORD

...that we are unable to intelligently consider Mind unless it is *embodied* in living substance. The idea of Mind, independent of its physical <note>original: "substantial"</note> embodiment, becomes a mere abstraction that is impossible to visualize—something like color independent of a colored object, or light without an illuminated substance. 

And just as we find that mental states influence physical conditions, so do we find that physical conditions influence mental states. Consequently, the problem of life, health, and disease once more loses its simplicity, and the mystery deepens again. The deeper we dig into the subject, the more we are impressed by the universal principle of action and reaction that is so apparent in all phenomena <note>original: "phenomena"; here referring to observable events or natural occurrences.</note>. The mind acts upon the body; the body reacts upon the mind. Cause and effect become blurred, and the reasoning becomes circular—like a ring, it has no beginning and no end. Its beginning may be any place we prefer, and its ending likewise.

The only reconciliation is to be found in the fundamental working hypothesis <term>working hypothesis: A theory used as a starting point for further study, even if it has not yet been proven.</term> which holds that both mind and body—both men-

<summary>Atkinson argues that mind and body are inextricably linked through a process of constant action and reaction, making it impossible to study one without the other.</summary>
<keywords>mind-body connection, action and reaction, health and disease, circular reasoning, working hypothesis</keywords>

<page-num>12</page-num>

-> # MIND AND BODY <-

mental states and physical conditions—*are the two aspects of something greater than either—the opposing poles of the same Reality.* The radical Materialist <term>Materialist: A philosopher who believes that only physical matter exists and that thoughts and feelings are merely physical processes of the brain.</term> asserts that the Body is the only reality, and that Mind is merely its “by-product.” The Mentalist <term>Mentalist: In this context, a philosopher who believes that the mind or spirit is the only true reality and the physical body is just an expression of it.</term> asserts that the Mind is the only reality, and that the Body is merely its grosser form of manifestation. The unprejudiced philosopher is likely <note>original: "apt"</note> to stand aside and say: “You are both right, yet both wrong—each is stating the truth, but only the half-truth.” With the working hypothesis that Mind and Body are but varying aspects of the Truth—that Mind is the inner essence of the Body, and Body the outward manifestation of the Mind—we find ourselves on safe ground.

We mention this fundamental principle here, because <note>original: "for"</note> in the main part of this book we shall not invade the territory <note>original: "province"</note> of metaphysics or philosophy, but shall hold ourselves firmly to our own field, that of psychology. Of course, the very nature of the subject makes it <note>original: "renders it"</note> necessary that we consider the influence of psychology upon physiology, but we have remembered that this book belongs to the gen- <meta>The fragment "gen-" is likely the beginning of the word "general," which would be completed on the next page.</meta>

<summary>Atkinson proposes a middle ground between Materialism and Mentalism, arguing that mind and body are two aspects of one reality. He clarifies that the book will focus on the practical application of psychology rather than abstract philosophical debate.</summary>

<keywords>Materialism, Mentalism, Mind-Body Connection, Psychology, Physiology, Metaphysics, Reality</keywords>

<header>FOREWORD</header>

general subject of the New Psychology <note>New Psychology: A late 19th and early 20th-century movement that applied psychological principles to self-improvement and healing, often associated with "New Thought."</note>, and we have accordingly emphasized the psychological side of the subject. But the same material could have been used by a writer on physiology <note>physiology: The branch of biology that deals with the normal functions of living organisms and their parts.</note> by changing the emphasis from the psychological phase to the physiological.

We have written this book to reach not only those who refuse to see the wonderful influence of mental states over physical conditions, but also for our "metaphysical" <note>metaphysical: Here, the author refers to spiritual or "New Thought" practitioners who believed the mind or spirit was the only reality.</note> friends who have become so captivated with the power of the mind that they practically ignore the existence of the body—indeed, in some cases, actually denying the existence of the latter. We believe that there is a sane middle ground in "metaphysical healing," just as there is in the material treatment of disease. In this case, not only does truth lie between the two extremes, but it is composed of the blending and assimilation <note>original: "assimilation"; the process of absorbing and integrating ideas.</note> of the two opposing ideas and theories. But, even if the reader does not fully agree with us in our general theories and conclusions, he will find within the covers of this book a mass of *facts* which he may use in building up a new theory of his own. And, after all,

# CHAPTER I

## THE SUBCONSCIOUS MIND

In order to understand the nature of the mind's influence upon the body—specifically the effect of mental states upon physical functions—we must understand the remarkable field of mental activity known in the New Psychology <note>The "New Psychology" refers to the movement in the late 19th and early 20th centuries that began applying experimental and scientific methods to the study of the mind.</note> as "The Subconscious Mind." This field has been called various names by different writers, such as the "Subjective Mind," the "Involuntary Mind," the "Subliminal Mind" <note>original: "Subliminal" — derived from the Latin *sub* (below) and *limen* (threshold), meaning below the threshold of conscious awareness.</note>, and the "Unconscious Mind." These different names exist because the investigation and classification of this subject are still relatively new.

Among the various functions of the subconscious mind, one of the most important is its management <note>original: "charge"</note> and control of the body's involuntary activities and functions. It performs this work through the sympathetic nervous system, the cells, and groups of cells. As all students of physiology know, the

<vocab>Subconscious Mind, New Psychology, Subjective Mind, Involuntary Mind, Subliminal Mind, Unconscious Mind, sympathetic nervous system, physiology</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the concept of the subconscious mind and its various academic names, identifying its primary role as the controller of the body's involuntary functions.</summary>
<keywords>subconscious mind, involuntary functions, sympathetic nervous system, New Psychology, mind-body connection</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the subconscious mind's control over biological processes, citing various 19th-century psychologists and philosophers to support the theory that mental states govern physical health.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>

<header>MIND AND BODY</header>

The majority of the body's activities are involuntary—meaning they are independent, or mostly independent, of the control of the conscious will. As Dr. Schofield <note>Dr. Alfred Taylor Schofield (1846–1933) was a prominent British physician who wrote extensively on the "unconscious mind" and its role in health before Freudian psychology became the standard.</note> says: 

> “The unconscious mind shares three qualities with the conscious mind—namely <note>original: "viz.", an abbreviation of the Latin *videlicet*</note>, will, intellect, and emotion—but it undoubtedly possesses another very important one: nutrition, or the general maintenance of the body.”

As Hudson <note>Thomas Jay Hudson (1834–1903) was an influential American author who popularized the "dual mind" theory, distinguishing between the objective (conscious) and subjective (subconscious) minds.</note> states: 

> “The subjective mind has absolute control of the functions, conditions, and sensations of the body.”

Despite the debate that is still raging concerning the exact nature of the subconscious mind, the authorities all agree on one fact: whatever else it may be, it can be considered the phase, aspect, part, or field of the mind that is in charge of and controls most of the body's physical functioning.

Von Hartmann <note>Eduard von Hartmann (1842–1906) was a German philosopher whose work, *Philosophy of the Unconscious*, bridge the gap between biological processes and mental activity.</note> says: 

> “The explanation that unconscious mental <note>original: "psychical"</note> activity itself properly shapes and maintains the body is not only logically sound, but it is also supported by every possible parallel from the various branches of physical and animal [life]...”

<summary>This page cites early psychological authorities to argue that the subconscious mind is responsible for the body's involuntary functions and physical maintenance.</summary>
<keywords>Dr. Schofield, Thomas Jay Hudson, Eduard von Hartmann, subconscious mind, involuntary functions, mind-body connection, subjective mind, nutrition</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the scientific consensus regarding the subconscious mind's influence over the body, citing several prominent 19th-century physicians and psychologists.</meta>

<page-num>17</page-num>

<header>SUBCONSCIOUS MIND</header>

...life in its favor, and it appears to be as scientifically certain as is possible when drawing conclusions from effect to cause.” Maudsley <note>Henry Maudsley (1835–1918) was a pioneering British psychiatrist who argued for the physical basis of mental processes.</note> says:

> “The connection between the mind and body is such that a specific mental state tends to immediately reflect itself within the body.”

Carpenter <note>William Benjamin Carpenter (1813–1885) was an English physiologist and psychologist known for his work on "unconscious cerebration," an early concept of the subconscious.</note> says:

> “If a mental state <note>original: “psychosis.” In 19th-century medical terms, this referred broadly to any mental process rather than a loss of reality.</note> is produced by a physical nerve state <note>original: “neurosis.” In this era, this term referred to a physical condition of the nerves rather than a psychological disorder.</note>—just as a pinprick causes pain—it is also true that a mental state can produce a physical nerve state. It is just as certain that mental causes lead to physical results as it is that physical causes lead to mental results.”

Tuke <note>Daniel Hack Tuke (1827–1895) was a specialist in mental illness and author of "Illustrations of the Influence of the Mind upon the Body."</note> says:

> “The mind, through the sensory, motor, blood-regulating <note>original: “vaso-motor”</note>, and growth-regulating <note>original: “trophic”</note> nerves, causes changes in sensation, muscular contraction, nutrition, and secretion. . . . If the brain is an outgrowth from a biological cell <note>original: “body corpuscle”</note> and is directly related to the structures and tissues that existed before it, then—even though those tissues continue their own functions—we must expect the brain to act upon the muscular tissue, the organic functions, and the nervous system itself.”

Von Hartmann <note>Eduard von Hartmann (1842–1906), a German philosopher who argued that the "Unconscious" was the primary force in both biology and psychology.</note> also says:

> “In willing any conscious act, the unconscious will is called forth <note>original: “evoked”</note>...”

<summary>This page quotes 19th-century medical authorities—Maudsley, Carpenter, Tuke, and Von Hartmann—to argue that mental states directly cause physical changes in the body. It establishes the "scientific certainty" of the mind-body connection through the nervous system.</summary>

<keywords>Henry Maudsley, William Benjamin Carpenter, Daniel Hack Tuke, Eduard von Hartmann, mind-body connection, subconscious, psychosis, neurosis, nervous system, physiology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between conscious mental effort and involuntary bodily functions, specifically how the "unconscious will" acts as a bridge between the two.</meta>

<header>MIND AND BODY</header>

...to establish the means to bring about an effect. For example, if I desire a stronger flow of saliva <note>original: "salivary secretion"</note>, the conscious act of willing this effect stimulates the unconscious will <note>The "unconscious will" refers to the biological processes that the mind controls without our active awareness.</note> to set the necessary physical means in motion. It is said that mothers are able to produce a more abundant flow of milk through their will, if the sight of their child arouses in them the desire to nurse. There are even people who can sweat <note>original: "perspire"</note> voluntarily. I now possess the power to instantly silence even the most severe hiccups <note>original: "hiccoughs"</note> through my own will, whereas they were previously a major source of inconvenience for me. . . . An urge to cough, which has no physical <note>original: "mechanical"</note> cause, can be permanently suppressed by the will. I believe we might possess much greater voluntary power over our bodily functions if we were only accustomed from childhood to conduct experiments and practice these techniques.

. . . We have arrived at the conclusion that every action of the mind on the body, without exception, is only possible by means of an unconscious will; such an unconscious will can be called forth partly by means of a conscious will, and partly also through the conscious idea of the effect, without con—

# CHAPTER II

## THE SYMPATHETIC SYSTEM

The average person has a general understanding of what is meant by "the nervous system." However, inquiry shows that by this term, people usually include only that part of the nervous system known as the "cerebro-spinal system." This is the system of nerves consisting of the brain and spinal cord, and the nerves extending from them throughout the body, the functions <note>original: "offices"</note> of which are to control the voluntary movements of the body. 

The average person is almost entirely unaware of the existence of the **Great Sympathetic System** <note>Now more commonly known as the Autonomic Nervous System.</note>, which controls involuntary movements and processes. These include the processes and functions of nutrition, secretion, reproduction, excretion, and vaso-motor action <note>The regulation of the contraction or dilation of blood vessels.</note>. In physiology, the term "sympathetic" is used to mean the "reciprocal action of the different parts of the body on each other; an affection of one part of the

<summary>This page introduces the Sympathetic Nervous System, distinguishing it from the better-known cerebro-spinal system by highlighting its role in controlling involuntary bodily functions like nutrition and circulation.</summary>
<keywords>nervous system, cerebro-spinal system, Great Sympathetic System, involuntary movements, physiology, anatomy, vaso-motor action</keywords>

> body in consequence of something taking place in another. Thus when there is a local injury, the whole frame after a time suffers with it. A wound anywhere will tend to create feverishness everywhere; derangement of the stomach will tend to produce headache, liver complaint to produce pain in the shoulder, etc."

An old authority thus describes the **Sympathetic Nerves**: "A system of nerves, running from the base of the skull to the coccyx <note>The tailbone.</note>, along both sides of the body, and consisting of a series of ganglia <note>A cluster of nerve cell bodies.</note> along the spinal column by the side of the vertebrae <note>The individual bones that make up the spine.</note>. With this trunk of the sympathetic system, there are communicating branches which connect the ganglia, or the intermediate cord, with all the spinal and several of the cranial <note>Related to the skull.</note> nerves proceeding to primary branches on the neighboring organs or other ganglia, and finally numerous flexures <note>original: "flexures"; curves or networks.</note> of nerves running to the viscera <note>The internal organs in the main cavities of the body, especially those in the abdomen.</note>. Various fibers from the sympathetic communicate with those of the cerebro-spinal system. In the New Psychology <note>A late 19th and early 20th-century movement that emphasized the study of the subconscious and its influence on physical health.</note>, the Sympathetic Nervous System is recognized as that which is directly under the control of the Subconscious Mind.

The Cerebro-Spinal Nervous System <note>The part of the nervous system consisting of the brain and spinal cord, responsible for voluntary actions and sensory processing.</note> is concerned with activities arising from the conscious activities of the mind, including those of the five senses. It controls the muscles by which we speak, walk, move our limbs, and pursue the ordinary activities of outer life. But, while these are very important to the individual, there is another set of activities—inner activities—which are nonetheless important. 

The Sympathetic System controls the involuntary muscles by means of which the heart throbs, the arteries pulsate, the air is conveyed to the lungs, and the blood moves to and from the heart. It also operates the various glands and tubes of the body, and performs the entire work of nutrition, repair, and body-building. While the Cerebro-Spinal System and the Conscious Mind are able to rest for a considerable portion of the twenty-four hours of the day, the Sympathetic System and the Subconscious Mind

The nerve centers of the Cerebro-Spinal System are grouped closely together, while those of the Sympathetic System are scattered throughout the body, each organ having its appropriate center or tiny-brain. The heart, the liver, the kidneys, the spleen, the brain, the intestinal tract, the bladder, and the reproductive <note>original: "generative"</note> organs each have their own particular nerve center of the Sympathetic System—each its own tiny-brain—each, however, connected with all the others. # CHAPTER III

## THE CELL-MINDS

Modern science has demonstrated that the human body is composed of a multitude of microscopic cells; that is, the muscles, nerves, tissues, blood, bones, hair, and nails are all made up of tiny cells and groups of cells. Rudolf Virchow <note>Rudolf Virchow (1821–1902) was a German physician and pathologist, often called the "father of modern pathology" for his pioneering work in cellular theory.</note> says: "It is from the cells that the tissues are built up and the nerves are formed. There is no part of the human body in which the cell is not seen. All these cells are nucleated <note>original: "neuclated"</note>—meaning they have within them a central life-spot <note>The nucleus, which contains the cell's genetic material.</note> like the yolk of an egg. Each cell is born, reproduces itself, dies, and is absorbed. The maintenance of life and health depends upon the constant regeneration of these cells. When man can control the life and death of the cell, he becomes the creator." Medical science now essentially claims <note>original: "practically asserts"</note> that disease of the body is really a disease of the cells of which the body is composed, and that all healing of the body must consist of the...

# CHAPTER V

## THE HISTORY OF PSYCHOTHERAPY <note>original: "PSYCHO-THERAPY"</note>

One of the most remarkable achievements of the "New Psychology" <note>A late 19th-century movement that sought to apply scientific methods to the study of the mind and the subconscious.</note> is its gathering of scattered examples showing how the mind's power affects the body. Throughout history, these instances have appeared under various masks and guises. The New Psychology unites them into one broad and general synthesis. Within this synthesis, we can see a single fundamental principle of mental healing operating under a thousand different names, forms, and theories, across every race, nation, and region <note>original: "clime"</note> in all ages, past and present. 

The New Psychology serves as the great reconciler of the various theories, dogmas, and speculations regarding the strange cures performed <note>original: "effected"</note> by the mind. It also explains the equally strange, adverse effects that negative thoughts can have on the physical body <note>original: "organism"</note>.

From the earliest days of history, we find records of strange and marvelous cures performed by non-material agents. In some...

...mentioned, testifying to the remarkable extent to which psychotherapy, in one form or another, was utilized in the countries of the ancient world. This, of course, does not necessarily imply that the ancients had any real understanding of the psychological and physiological principles governing its operation. On the contrary, there is every reason to believe that they used it much as too many of the mental healers of today do—on the basis of "faith healing" pure and simple, with no attempt at diagnosis and in a hit-or-miss fashion. 

It was not until the very end of the Middle Ages, so far as history informs us, that anything even remotely resembling a scientific inquiry into its nature and possibilities was undertaken. Even then, it was only in a faint, vague, and indefinite way, conducted by men who were metaphysicians and mystics rather than scientists. The first of these, Pietro Pomponazzi <note>original: "Petrys Pomponatius"; an Italian philosopher (1462–1525) who argued that many seemingly "miraculous" cures were actually the result of natural forces and the power of the human mind.</note>, a sixteenth-century philosopher, sought to prove that disease was curable without drugs by means of the "magnetism" <note>term: magnetism; in this context, it refers to an early belief in a vital fluid or invisible force that could be passed from a healer to a patient to restore health.</note> existing in certain specially gifted individuals. Alexander Bertrand, a Frenchman, published a work about this time, holding theories similar to those of Faria <note>Abbé Faria (1756–1819) was a priest who pioneered the scientific study of hypnotism, arguing that the power of the "trance" came from the mind of the patient, not from a "magnetic fluid" in the healer.</note>. In 1841, James Braid, an English physician, becoming interested in Mesmerism <note>Mesmerism: a system of healing developed by Franz Mesmer based on the idea of "animal magnetism," an invisible natural force in the body.</note>, discovered that the mesmeric state might be artificially induced by staring at bright objects until the eyes became fatigued, and so on. At the same time, as we shall now see, Mental Healing has been attracting much attention in other ways, outside of the medical profession, and often allied with religious and metaphysical movements. To understand the subject, we must study it in all of its phases.

In the early part of the nineteenth century, Elijah Perkins <note>The author likely refers to Elisha Perkins (1741–1799). Perkins <note>The text continues from the previous page: "people flocked to Perkins..."</note> from far and near in order to receive the benefit of his wonderful treatments. The popularity of the system spread rapidly in the United States, particularly in New England, with every city and many towns supporting <note>original: "patronizing"</note> Perkins’ practitioners and healers. From this country the craze spread to Great Britain, and even to the European continent.

Centers of treatment, and even hospitals, were established by the “Perkinsites,” and the fame of the tractors <note>A pair of small metal rods, usually brass and steel, used to stroke the body to "draw out" disease.</note> increased daily in ever-widening circles. The exposure of this experiment, and the general acceptance of the explanation for these occurrences <note>original: "phenomena"</note>, caused "Perkinsism" to fade away quickly. Today, it is mentioned only in the context of medical history and in books focused on the influence of the mind over the body.

The striking peculiarity of these cures is that they all occur during the height of the excitement and notoriety following the initial announcement. They then decline in frequency as public faith and interest fade. The explanation is that, in every instance, the cure is brought about by the patient's mental state—specifically their expectancy, faith, and imagination—regardless of any actual merit <note>original: "virtue"</note> in the method or system itself. 

In short, all these cures belong to the category of **faith-cures**. They are merely duplicates of the age-old cures resulting from faith in sacred relics, shrines, the bones of holy people, and sacred places, of which nearly every religion has provided many examples. The history of medicine offers many instances of the effectiveness of the therapeutic power of Faith.

Sir Humphry Davy <note>Sir Humphry Davy (1778–1829) was a famous British chemist and inventor. This story is a classic example of what we now call the "placebo effect."</note> tells of a case in which a seriously ill man showed immediate improvement after a clinical thermometer was placed in his mouth, because he supposed it was some powerful new healing instrument. This healing results from faith, which is the active force in all the other cases mentioned. The specific theology, religious creed, or theories of method have very little to do with the cures, so long as the right amount of faith is created in the patient's mind. Faith in anything will work cures, provided it is intense and active enough.

### HISTORY OF PSYCHO-THERAPY

Another branch of Mental Healing is found in the modern schools of "New Thought," "Mental Science," "Christian Science," and the "Emmanuel Movement" <note>An Episcopal movement launched in 1906 that combined psychological counseling with spiritual healing.</note>. Scholars generally agree that these various schools originated from the widespread interest in the subject that appeared in the United States and Great Britain around the middle of the 19th century <note>original: "last century," referring to the 1800s</note>. 

Some experts believe this general interest was largely triggered by the teachings of Charles Poyen, a Frenchman who moved from France to New England around 1835. He brought with him French teachings and theories regarding mesmerism <term>mesmerism: an early form of hypnotism named after Franz Mesmer, based on the idea of "animal magnetism" flowing between people</term> and related phenomena. Poyen’s teachings attracted significant interest and attention, and he soon gained a large number of followers, students, and imitators. 

Teachers of this "new science" appeared everywhere. Many theories were developed and actively supported by the followers of these various prominent teachers. The growing interest in phrenology <note>A popular 19th-century pseudoscience involving the study of the shape and size of the skull as an indication of character and mental abilities.</note> and the emerging interest in spiritualism helped spread the new teachings regarding mesmerism, clairvoyance <note>The supposed ability to perceive things or events in the future or beyond normal sensory contact.</note>, psychic healing, and other similar topics.

The decade from 1840 to 1850 witnessed a remarkable interest in psychic phenomena of all kinds. During that time, the foundations were undoubtedly laid upon which later systems have since been built. Anyone reading the short stories of Edgar Allan Poe <note>1809–1849; American writer known for his tales of mystery and the macabre, which often explored themes of mesmerism and the subconscious.</note> and other writers of that period can easily see the high level of public interest in these subjects at the time.

Most authorities agree that in Phineas Parkhurst Quimby <note>1802–1866; an American clockmaker and healer whose ideas about the relationship between belief and health became the foundation for the "New Thought" movement.</note>, we have the direct link between that earlier period and the present day. Quimby played a very important role in the evolution of modern concepts of **mental healing**, or <term>psychotherapy</term> <note>The treatment of mental or physical disorders by psychological rather than medical means.</note> as it is now called. He was a poor clockmaker with very limited financial resources, but he possessed a good character and a strong personality. He began to experiment on the people in his town, and as a result, he soon acquired a reputation as a powerful mesmerist and a talented healer. Quimby, thinking deeply regarding the cures he was making, soon concluded that while his *cures* were genuine, his theories were wrong. He held that all that is required to achieve a cure is to bring about "a change of thought." To treat them, he simply sat by the side of the afflicted person, picturing them as well and whole, and impressing upon the patient’s mind that they were healthy and complete, *in Truth* <note>In this context, "Truth" refers to a metaphysical reality where disease does not exist, a core concept in mental healing traditions.</note>. From this fundamental idea, he gradually evolved a philosophy that has strongly influenced later schools of thought. Quimby spoke often about his great "discovery," as he called it, and built great hopes on establishing "the science of health and happiness." 

He began to speak of the "Truth" in his "science," which he believed was identical to what Christ taught, and the means by which Jesus performed his miraculous cures. However, before he had firmly established his "science," he died. He left his work to be carried on by others—notably by Dr. Warren F. Evans and Julius A. Dresser. These men should be given the credit for launching what is now known as the "New Thought Movement" <term>New Thought Movement</term> <note>A spiritual movement that developed in the United States in the 19th century, emphasizing the power of positive thinking and the idea that the mind can heal the body.</note>.

Mrs. Mary Baker G. Eddy, who afterward established "Christian Science" <term>Christian Science</term> <note>A religious movement founded by Mary Baker Eddy in 1879, which teaches that sickness is an illusion that can be overcome by prayer and a specific understanding of God.</note>, was one of Quimby’s patients and students. Dresser and others have positively stated and claimed that she received her initial ideas from him...

# CHAPTER VI

## FAITH CURES

After scientific studies examined the phenomena of curing physical illnesses through the power of mental states, researchers recognized that a common principle was at work across many different forms and appearances. This led to the scientific adoption of the term "Faith Cures." This term was used to describe every instance and method of healing that falls under the general category of mental healing <term>Mental Healing</term> <note>A broad term for various systems that use the mind to treat physical or nervous disorders, popular in the late 19th and early 20th centuries.</note>.

Professor Goddard <note>Likely Henry H. Goddard (1866–1957), a prominent American psychologist of the era known for his work on the relationship between the mind and physical health.</note> defines the term as follows: 

"A term applied to the practice of curing disease by appealing to the hope, belief, or expectation of the patient, and without the use of drugs or other material means. Formerly, it was restricted to methods requiring the exercise of religious faith—such as the 'prayer cure' and 'divine healing'—but it has now come to be used in a broader sense. It now includes the cures achieved through 'Mental Science' <term>Mental Science</term> <note>A movement related to New Thought that emphasized the power of the mind and "right thinking" to heal the body.</note> and hypnotism; it also includes a..."

<summary>This page introduces the scientific definition of "Faith Cures" as a broad category for all mental healing practices that rely on a patient's hope and expectation rather than medicine. It notes that while the term once only referred to religious healing, it now includes secular practices like hypnotism and Mental Science.</summary>

...familiar occurrences seem to justify an affirmative answer. It is well known that certain glands and secretions are markedly affected by emotions. Happiness aids digestion, while unhappiness slows it down. <term>Mosso</term> <note>Angelo Mosso (1846–1910) was an Italian physiologist who studied the physical effects of fear and fatigue on the body.</note> has demonstrated that the bladder is especially sensitive to emotional states. 

In general, pleasant emotions produce the opposite physical effect of unpleasant ones. There are many glands within the body whose actions under the influence of emotion we cannot observe directly, but we may reasonably assume that they are also affected by emotional states. Hence, if unpleasant emotions act upon the glands in such a way as to disrupt the body's systems and cause disease, then pleasant emotions may reasonably be assumed to help restore normal functions.

Seasick persons have been relieved of their nausea by being made to bail out a leaking boat due to the fear that it would sink. All their attention was thereby diverted from themselves. Many can recall how children—and grown persons, too—have forgotten all about their supposedly intense thirst as soon as their attention was diverted. Some persons, after eating something which they fancy is a trifle indigestible, center their attention upon the stomach, expecting symptoms of indigestion, and are often not disappointed.

It is simply necessary to get a patient to think the proper thoughts; the work of the therapist lies in directing those thoughts.

## CHAPTER VII

### THE POWER OF THE IMAGINATION

Dr. F. W. Southworth says: “Fear itself is a contagious disease and is sometimes reflected from one mind to another with great rapidity. It passes from one person to another—from the healthy to the sick <note>original: "ill"</note>, from doctor or nurse to patient, from mother to child, and so on. We can usually escape the greatest fears, but it is the small fears and anxieties, the constant apprehension, and the fears of all sorts of imagined evils that drain our vitality and weaken our powers, thus making us more susceptible to disease. 

To prevent <note>original: "avert"</note> disease, then, we must eliminate fear; but how shall we achieve it? Through wise education—teaching people a higher standard of living; by teaching better hygiene, a wiser philosophy, and a more cheerful theology <note>In this context, Atkinson refers to religious outlooks that emphasize hope and love over fear and punishment</note>. We must achieve this by erasing a thousand errors and superstitions from fearful minds and guiding them toward the light.”

<vocab>Dr. F. W. Southworth, fear, imagination, hygiene, philosophy, theology, education</vocab>

<summary>This page defines fear as a "contagious disease" that spreads between individuals and weakens the body's natural defenses against illness. When they were attached, there were no marks of any kind on the patient’s body, but at the expected time, the "stigmata" were visible as usual through the glass.

The author has been informed by a prominent physician in Chicago that for many years he has been in the habit of giving hypodermic injections of distilled water while telling the patient he is injecting morphine. ...to have been clearly stated <note>This text continues the sentence from the previous page: "It is one of the most straightforward things in the world. The attempt to plant <note>original: fix</note> an idea in the mind without relying on reason is called suggestion. In medical practice, this is usually accomplished by asking the patient to lie down and relax both body and mind, and then firmly stating the desired idea to them. 

It can be achieved in several ways. For instance, a patient might be told that the practitioner <note>original: operator</note> is a wizard who is about to transfer an idea from his own mind into the patient’s. If the patient believes him, they will very likely accept the idea. It can also be done through gestures or chants <note>original: incantations</note> that the patient views with superstitious awe, provided it is explained beforehand what these gestures are intended to achieve. It may even be accomplished by telling the patient they have no body and sitting with them for a while in spiritual silence, *as long as the patient knows what to expect.*

...that is based on a certain truth is the method of the scientist. Suggestive therapeutics <note>a branch of medicine focused on treating disease through the use of suggestion rather than drugs</note> is the use of suggestion to establish healthy mental habits in the mind. . . .

> "Our question is: Can the physical conditions of the brain affect the physical state of the stomach? We know that the brain activity <note>original: brain-building condition</note> that accompanies the idea of raising our hand can affect the state of the muscles in our arm—and we call that a voluntary function <note>an action performed by conscious choice</note>. Now the question is whether the brain state accompanying the idea of energizing <note>original: enlivening</note> our stomach can affect that involuntary function <note>a bodily process, like digestion, that happens automatically</note>. Experiments with suggestion have proved that in some cases it can, if it continues long enough. People who are very suggestible <note>easily influenced by the ideas or words of others</note> can, for instance,"

# CHAPTER IX

## PSYCHOTHERAPEUTIC METHODS

Readers will have seen from the preceding chapters that we have proceeded based on the theory that suggestion is the universal working principle <note>original: operative principle</note> manifest in all forms of mental healing, regardless of the appearance the healing takes or the method used to apply it. But it must be remembered that by "suggestion," we do not mean the theories of any particular group of psychotherapists <note>original: psycho-therapists</note>. Rather, we mean the broad general principle that influences the subconscious mind and its activities. Let us consider the principle of suggestion so that we may understand what it is and what it is not.

The term "suggestion" has as its root the Latin word "to carry under" <note>original: *suggero*</note>, which is translated as follows: "under" <note>original: *sug* or *sub*</note> and "to carry" <note>original: *gero*</note>. That is, it literally means "to carry or place [under]"...

<vocab>Suggestion, psychotherapeutic methods, subconscious mind, Latin etymology</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces Chapter IX by defining "suggestion" as the fundamental principle underlying all mental healing, regardless of the specific method used. Atkinson explains that the word literally means to "carry under," referring to how ideas are placed into the subconscious mind.</summary>

<keywords>suggestion, psychotherapeutic methods, subconscious mind, etymology, mental healing, operative principle</keywords>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page continues the definition of "suggestion," transitioning from its Latin etymology to its practical application in "New Psychology" and medical therapeutics. It explains how suggestions bypass the conscious mind to influence the subconscious mind and physical health.</meta>

<page-num>174</page-num>
<header>MIND AND BODY</header>

under.” In its general usage, it signifies “the indirect introduction of an idea into the mind or thoughts, or the idea itself that is introduced.” Ordinarily, a “suggestion” is an idea indirectly planted <note>original: insinuated</note> in the mind, usually without the process of argument or reasoning. In the New Psychology <note>A late 19th and early 20th-century movement that sought to apply scientific methods to the study of the mind and the subconscious.</note>, the term “suggestion” is used to mean an idea which is “carried under” the objective or conscious mind and introduced to the subjective or subconscious mind. In Suggestive Therapeutics <note>A branch of medicine popular in the early 1900s that used the power of suggestion to treat physical and nervous disorders.</note>, a “suggestion” is an idea introduced into that part of the subconscious mind which governs and controls physical functions and activities—the mind that is embodied in the cells and cell-groups of the body, as we stated in the preceding chapters.

Many mental healers apply the term “Suggestion” only to the particular method of applying suggestion employed by physicians and others who practice under the general theories of Suggestive Therapeutics. Consequently, these healers deny that they use Suggestion because, as they claim, they do not use the methods of the practitioners of...

<header>PSYCHO-THERAPEUTIC METHODS</header>

Suggestive Therapeutics <note>A branch of medicine popular in the early 1900s that used the power of suggestion to treat physical and nervous disorders.</note>, and achieve their cures through "metaphysical" <note>In this context, "metaphysical" refers to systems of thought, like New Thought, that emphasize the power of mind over matter.</note> or "spiritual" means, or according to some creed or metaphysical theory which, once accepted, brings about the cure. We believe that the impartial reader who has followed us this far will see that these metaphysical theories, creeds, and specific dogmas are simply the outward mask of Suggestion. These healers simply provide a form of Suggestion that is acceptable to the patient because of his temperament, training, and background, and the healing process operates along the lines of the "faith cure" <note>A healing method based on the patient's belief in a divine or spiritual power.</note>.

The fact that healers with entirely opposite theories and doctrines manage to achieve cures in roughly the same proportion and in about the same amount of time would seem to prove that the theories or dogmas have very little to do with the actual work of healing. Whichever form of Suggestion is most acceptable to the patient will best perform the healing work in that particular case. This also serves to explain why some patients, failing to find relief from one school of mental healing, are often cured by healers of another—

<summary>Atkinson argues that all forms of mental and spiritual healing, regardless of their specific religious or philosophical doctrines, are actually powered by the universal principle of suggestion. He explains that the effectiveness of a particular "cure" depends more on the patient's belief in the method than on the truth of the healer's theories.</summary>

Other schools of metaphysical <note>In this context, "metaphysical" refers to spiritual or philosophical healing movements like Christian Science or New Thought, which were popular in the early 20th century.</note> or religious healers treat the patient by impressing upon their mind the idea that because God is perfect, good, and loving, He could not be responsible for creating evil, pain, or disease. The "Mental Scientists" <note>Mental Science was a branch of the New Thought movement which emphasized that the universe is composed of "mind stuff" and that humans can change their physical reality by changing their thoughts.</note> come closest to the ideas of the New Psychology <note>In 1912, "New Psychology" referred to the emerging scientific study of the subconscious mind and its influence on the physical body.</note> when they teach that "as a person thinks, so they are" <note>original: "As a man thinketh, so is he," a reference to Proverbs 23:7</note> and that the human mind creates physical conditions, both good and evil. Differing from these metaphysical, mystical, or religious schools of healing in theory—though employing the same underlying principle—we find the school of Suggestive Therapeutics. <note>Suggestive Therapeutics is the clinical application of suggestion, often by a physician or psychologist, to treat physical or nervous disorders without necessarily invoking spiritual or religious doctrines.</note>

...will change the current condition to the one desired is always the correct choice, and the practitioner's own intelligence will be the best guide as to what the suggestion should be. . . . Always arouse the <term>expectant attention</term> <note>Expectant attention refers to a psychological state where the patient is focused and waiting for a specific physical or mental result to occur, which makes them more receptive to healing.</note> of a patient. . . . A line of argument so logical can be developed that each patient will have a clear reason for expecting certain conditions to be brought about. *With the patient’s attention on the desired results, they generally come to pass.* It is better not to give <term>negative suggestions</term> <note>Negative suggestions are instructions that focus on what the patient should avoid or stop feeling (e.g., "You won't feel pain"), which can accidentally keep the patient's mind focused on the problem.</note>, such as, ‘You will not, or cannot do this, that, or the other thing,’ etc. Pointing out what is undesirable is not enough. In place of such suggestions, tell your patients what you actually wish them to do. For example, if a man were to get on his bicycle incorrectly, he would gain nothing if we simply told him that his way of mounting was wrong. How much easier it would be for everyone involved if the correct way to get on the bike were shown to him immediately. It is exactly the same with <term>therapeutic suggestions</term>; keep suggesting the mental or physical conditions you wish to bring about.”

It is worth noting that in the treatment described above, the suggestions are focused on <term>physiological</term> <note>Relating to the healthy or normal functioning of the body.</note> and <term>hygienic</term> <note>Relating to the maintenance of health and the prevention of disease through cleanliness and proper habits.</note> principles. In all scientific <term>suggestive treatment</term>, the aim is to create a mental image of the desired state rather than focusing on the current, undesirable symptoms. The ideal state is kept in focus, and the patient's mind is guided to realize this ideal—to make it a reality, to turn thoughts into actions, and to bring the mental picture to life.

The general principles of <term>Suggestive Therapeutics</term> <note>The practice of treating physical or mental illness by influencing the patient's mind through positive suggestions.</note> can be applied effectively through <term>Auto-Suggestion</term> <note>The process of influencing one's own mind or physical state through self-directed thoughts or "affirmations."</note>. In fact, the “affirmations,” “statements,” and “assertions” used by many <term>New Thought</term> <note>A late 19th-century philosophical movement that taught that "right thinking" has a healing effect on the body and can lead to personal success.</note> schools are simply different forms of auto-suggestion.

<page-num>193</page-num>

# PSYCHO-THERAPEUTIC METHODS

There is no fundamental difference between a suggestion given by others and an <term>Auto-Suggestion</term> <note>A suggestion that an individual gives to themselves to influence their own subconscious mind.</note> given by oneself to oneself. The healing power resides in the mind of the patient, and it does not matter whether it is called forth by their own auto-suggestion or the suggestion of a healer. Auto-suggestion is merely a case of self-healing by suggestion and is administered on the principle of "every person their own suggestionist"—or, as the old saying goes, "says I to myself, says I." 

Auto-suggestions are usually given to oneself in the form of <term>affirmations</term> <note>Positive, assertive statements used to program the mind for a specific physical or mental change.</note>, such as, "I am improving; my stomach is doing its work well, digesting what is given to it, and the nourishment is being assimilated, etc." In other works by this author, the method of addressing oneself as one would address another person is recommended as particularly effective <note>original: "efficacious"</note>. That is to say, instead of saying, "I am, etc.," in an auto-suggestion, it is better to address yourself in the **second person**, saying, "*John Smith* (naming yourself), *you are, etc.*" In short, the auto-suggestion seems to have additional strength given to it by being directed as if it were being given to another person.

<vocab>Suggestion, Auto-Suggestion, self-healing, affirmations, second person</vocab>

The following thought from <term>Dr. Schofield</term> <note>Dr. Alfred Taylor Schofield (1846–1933), a British physician and author known for his work on the relationship between the mind and physical health.</note> is worthy of careful consideration in connection with the methods of applying **suggestion**. Referring to the treatment of <term>hysterical disorders</term> <note>In the early 20th century, "hysteria" was a common medical diagnosis for a variety of physical symptoms thought to originate in the mind; today, these are often categorized as conversion or psychosomatic disorders.</note> and ailments, he says:

"We must, however, remember one great point with regard to suggestion—that it is like nitrogen. Nitrogen is the essential element in all animal life; it forms four-fifths of the air we breathe, and yet, strangely enough <note>original: "curious to say"</note>, we have no power to use it in a pure state. We can only take it in unconsciously when it is combined with other substances in the form of <term>protein food</term> <note>original: "proteid food"</note>.

It is the same with suggestions. Not one person suffering from hysteria in a hundred can receive and profit by them in a 'raw' state—that is, consciously. They must generally be presented, as we have said, indirectly to the **subconscious mind** through the treatment and environment of the patient. An electric shock often cures slight hysterical diseases instantaneously, acting, as it often does, on the unconscious mind through the conscious. No doubt it would be easier if we could say to these sufferers, 'The disease is caused by suggestions from <term>ideal centers</term> <note>Referring to the areas of the brain or mind where ideas and mental images are formed.</note>,

"...and to cure it, all you have to do is believe you are well.’ Still, just as it would be impossible for us to take our nitrogen pure from the air, the mind cannot, as a rule, be acted upon directly in this way when the brain is unhealthy. The methods that will bring about the best results must be carefully modeled upon the patient’s particular temperament, education, personal prejudices, and general beliefs. The skilled <term>suggestionist</term> <note>A practitioner who uses the power of suggestion to influence a patient's mental or physical state.</note> adapts his treatment and methods to every individual case he treats. Whatever method will best arouse the patient’s belief, faith, and <term>expectant attention</term> <note>A state of focused anticipation where the patient's mind is primed to receive and act upon a suggestion.</note> is the best method for administering suggestions. The successful practitioner must be “all things to all men,” while never losing sight of the fundamental principle of Suggestion—the arousing of faith, belief, and expectant attention.

## CHAPTER X

### **THE REACTION OF THE PHYSICAL**

As we mentioned in our Foreword, there is a constant action and reaction between mental states and physical conditions. In this book, due to the nature of our subject, we have started with the aspect of the mental state and worked from that point toward the consideration of the physical condition. In the same way, many <term>physiologists</term> <note>Scientists who study the biological functions and mechanisms of living organisms.</note> start from the aspect of the physical condition and work up to the mental state. However, regardless of which side one starts from, any honest investigator must admit that there is an endless chain of action and reaction between mind and body—and between body and mind.

This action and reaction works to build up the system as well as to tear it down. For instance, if a person’s mental states are positive, optimistic, cheerful, and uplifting, the body will respond and their physical conditions will improve. The physical condi-

<summary>This page introduces the concept of the reciprocal relationship between the mind and body, noting that mental states directly influence physical health and vice versa.</summary>
<keywords>action and reaction, mental states, physical conditions, physiologists, mind-body connection, optimism</keywords>

--- Page 203 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the reciprocal relationship between the mind and the body, explaining how improvements or declines in one area inevitably trigger a corresponding reaction in the other.</meta>

<page-num>197</page-num>

<header>REACTION OF THE PHYSICAL</header>

conditions, thus improving, will react upon mental states, giving them a clarity and strength greater than they previously showed. The improved mental state again acts upon the physical conditions, improving the latter still further. And so it goes, in an endless chain of cause and effect: each effect becomes a cause for a subsequent effect, and each cause arises from a preceding effect. 

Likewise, a depressed or harmful mental state will act upon physical conditions, which in turn will react upon mental states, and so on, in an endless chain of destructive cause and effect. It is a striking illustration of the old Biblical statement: "To him who has, more will be given; but from him who has not, even what he has will be taken away." <note>original: "To him who hath shall be given; to him who hath not shall be taken away even that which he hath." This quote from the Parable of the Talents (Matthew 25:29) is used here to describe how success—or health—tends to build upon itself, while decline leads to further loss.</note> 

In improving either the mental state or the physical condition, one gives an uplift to the whole process of action and reaction. Meanwhile, whatever adversely affects either the mental state or the physical condition starts a depressing and destructive process of action and reaction. The ideal to be aimed at is, of course, "A healthy mind in a healthy body" <note>original: "Mens sana in corpore sano," a famous phrase by the Roman poet Juvenal.</note>—and the two are so closely related that

Whatever affects one, favorably or unfavorably, is sure to react upon the other.

Just as the influence of mental states over physical conditions has been shown to operate by means of the sympathetic nervous system (controlled, of course, by the subconscious mind), the influence of physical conditions over mental states may also be explained in physiological terms. In order to understand the reaction of the body upon the mind, we only have to recall the fact that the subconscious mind is the builder and preserver of the very brain cells used by the conscious mind to express thought. 

Additionally, the entire nervous system—including both the cerebrospinal <term>The part of the nervous system consisting of the brain and spinal cord, which controls voluntary movements and sensory information.</term> and the sympathetic systems—is really under the control of the subconscious mind as far as growth and nourishment are concerned. The very brain and nerve centers through which thought, feeling, emotion, and will are expressed are nourished by the sympathetic system, and they are harmed by anything that affects that system. The sympathetic system joins all parts of the organism so closely together that trouble in one part is reflected

Herbert A. Parkyn, MD, clearly describes the action and reaction of the Mind and Body as follows: 

"A tree is much like a human being. Give it plenty of fresh air, water, and a rich soil, and it will flourish. To the same degree that it is deprived of these things, it wilts; and *the first part of the tree to wilt when nutrition becomes imperfect is the top. This is due to the force of gravity; the 'blood' of the tree—the sap—must overcome this force of nature when nourishing the highest leaves.* 

Human blood is also affected by this same force. The moment a person's circulation begins to decline because of limited nutrition, we find that the first symptoms of trouble appear in the head. . . . When the brain fails to receive its usual amount of blood, problems such as impaired memory, inability to concentrate, sleeplessness, nervousness, irritability, the blues <note>original: "the blues"; a common term for feelings of depression or low spirits</note>, and slight headaches develop. The impulses sent to all..."

...throughout the body become weaker, the various organs do not perform their functions as satisfactorily as usual. As the impulses sent to the stomach and bowels become weaker and weaker, indigestion <note>original: "dyspepsia"</note>, or constipation, or both, soon follow. As soon as these—the primary organs of nutrition—are out of order, overall nourishment fails rapidly and more "brain-related symptoms" <note>original: "head symptoms"</note> develop. 

Every impulse of the muscular system originates in the brain, and the strength of these impulses depends upon the nutrition provided to the brain centers controlling the various muscle groups. As the nutrition to these centers declines, the whole muscular system, including the muscles of the bowels, becomes weaker, and the patient complains that he exhausts easily. 

As the impulses for eliminating waste become weaker, waste products remain in the circulation. Any of the ailments which naturally follow this state of affairs—such as rheumatism, sick headaches, nausea <note>original: "biliousness"</note>, etc.—are likely to develop. As the centers of the special senses feel the lessening of the essential blood flow <note>original: "vital fluid"</note>, troubles such as impaired vision, impaired hearing, loss of appetite (the sense of taste), and an inability to detect odors begin to emerge.

Physiologists are able to provide a great variety of examples showing the effect of physical conditions over mental states. The term "hysteria" is derived from the Greek word meaning "the womb" or "uterus." <note>original: *hystera*. This reflects the ancient and now-discredited medical theory that hysterical symptoms were caused by disturbances in the womb.</note> Everyone knows the mental states produced by a sluggish liver, <term>dyspepsia</term> <note>chronic indigestion</note>, or constipation. We all know the difference between our mental capacity for thinking when we are tired compared to the clarity that accompanies a refreshed physical condition. No one, regardless of their philosophy, can truthfully claim to be able to maintain a calm, even disposition and a perfectly controlled temper when they are suffering from a painful boil on the back of their neck.

And everyone knows that after indulging in a late-night "Welsh rarebit" <note>A dish of savory melted cheese sauce served over toasted bread; historically, it was often blamed for causing indigestion and vivid nightmares.</note>, a person is likely to dream of their grandmother's ghost or see winged elephants in their sleep. We all know the delirium produced by overindulgence in alcohol and the hallucinations that accompany a fever. The effects of drugs, tobacco, and alcohol upon our mental states are well known. "Philip drunk" <note>A reference to an ancient story about Philip II of Macedon, suggesting that a person’s character and judgment change completely when intoxicated compared to when they are sober.</note> represents a very different mentality than "Philip sober." The mental states that accompany particular diseases are well known to physicians. One disease makes the sufferer prone to gloominess, while...

<summary>This page illustrates how physical ailments—ranging from minor irritations like boils to chronic issues like indigestion and intoxication—directly dictate a person's mood and mental clarity.</summary>
<keywords>hysteria, dyspepsia, physical-mental connection, delirium, Philip drunk, Welsh rarebit, medical history</keywords>

--- Page 211 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 204 regarding how specific physical diseases influence mental states, concluding the comparison between diseases that cause gloominess and those that cause excitement.</meta>

<page-num>205</page-num>

<header>->REACTION OF THE PHYSICAL<-</header>

...another will induce a state of feverish hilarity. Some leading authorities now believe that many cases of insanity are actually caused by abnormal conditions of the blood, rather than by any diseased condition of the brain itself.

One of the clearest examples of the action and reaction between mental states and physical conditions is found in the activities of the sexual system. Psychologists correctly maintain that sexual excesses and abnormalities are largely caused by improper thinking—that is, by allowing the attention and interest to dwell too intensely and continuously upon subjects connected with the activities of that part of the physical body. Mental treatment using Suggestive Therapeutics <note>A form of therapy based on the power of suggestion, often associated with early psychological practices and hypnotherapy.</note> has resulted in curing many people of these types of issues. 

But, notice the related fact: excesses and abnormalities of this kind almost always react upon the mental state of the person indulging in them. Conditions such as "softening of the brain" <note>original: "softening of the brain." An archaic medical term once used to describe various degenerative brain diseases, including the late stages of syphilis.</note>, paralysis, or severe cognitive impairment <note>original: "imbecility"</note> have often resulted directly from these physical abuses. It is clear that any sensible treatment for these troubles must take into consideration...

<summary>This page discusses how physical conditions, such as blood chemistry and sexual health, directly impact mental states, and argues that effective treatment must address both the mind and the body.</summary>

<keywords>insanity, blood conditions, brain health, sexual health, Suggestive Therapeutics, softening of the brain, mental-physical interaction</keywords>

--- Page 212 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 205 regarding the necessity of treating both the mind and the body, emphasizing their reciprocal relationship.</meta>

<page-num>206</page-num>

# MIND AND BODY

both Body and Mind. In the same way, it is a fact that just as certain mental states—particularly those of fear, worry, grief, jealousy, and so on—will harmfully affect the organs of digestion and nutrient absorption <note>original: "assimilation"</note>, so too will the poor functioning of these organs tend to produce depressing mental states similar to those just mentioned. Many instances of these strange correspondences are found in the study of physiological psychology or psychological physiology <note>These are early terms for the fields we now call biopsychology or neuroscience, which study how biological processes in the body relate to mental behavior and thought.</note>.

To more fully appreciate the relationship between the body and the mind, let us read the following lines from Professor Halleck <note>Reuben Post Halleck (1859–1936) was an influential American psychologist and educator whose work focused on the physiological basis of the mind.</note>:

> “However marvelous the mind’s achievements may be, we must note that it is as completely dependent upon the nervous system as a plant is upon sun, rain, and air. Suppose a child of intelligent parents were brought into the world with a perfect brain, yet without a single nerve leading from that brain to any portion of his body. Imagine him with no optic nerve <note>The nerve that carries visual information from the eye to the brain.</note> to transmit the glorious sensations of sight from the eye, no auditory nerve <note>The nerve responsible for hearing.</note> to conduct the vibrations of his mother’s voice, no tactile nerves <note>The nerves responsible for the sense of touch.</note> to convey the touch of a hand, and no olfactory nerve to...

<summary>This page explores the reciprocal relationship between mental states and physical health, specifically how emotions affect digestion and vice versa, while introducing a hypothetical scenario by Prof. Halleck to illustrate the mind's total dependence on the nervous system.</summary>

<keywords>mind-body connection, digestion, physiological psychology, Prof. Halleck, nervous system, sensory nerves, mental states</keywords>

--- Page 213 ---
<meta>This page continues the thought experiment from Professor Halleck on page 206, which imagines a child born without any sensory nerves to illustrate the mind's total dependence on the body's nervous system for information.</meta>

<page-num>207</page-num>

<header>REACTION OF THE PHYSICAL</header>

awaken the brain with the delicate aroma from the orchards and the wildflowers in spring, and no gustatory <note>original: "gustatory" — relating to the sense of taste</note>, thermal <note>relating to the sense of temperature</note>, or muscular nerves. Even if such a child survived as the years went by, the books of Shakespeare and Milton would be opened in vain before the child’s eyes. The wisest people might talk to him with the greatest eloquence, but it would all be useless. Nature could not whisper a single one of her inspiring truths into his deaf ears, nor could she light up that dark mind with a picture of a rainbow or of a human face. No matter how perfect the child’s brain and his inherited capacity for mental activities might be, his faculties would remain for this life shrouded in total darkness <note>original: "Egyptian darkness" — a biblical reference to the plague of darkness, used here to mean absolute, profound darkness</note>. Perception could give memory nothing to retain, and thought could not weave her matchless fabrics without materials.”

The very feelings or emotions themselves are so closely related to the accompanying physical expressions that it is difficult to distinguish between cause and effect, or indeed to state positively which one is really the cause of the other. Professor William [James] <note>William James (1842–1910) was a pioneering American psychologist and philosopher who proposed that emotions are the result of physical reactions to events, rather than the cause of them.</note>

<summary>This page concludes Professor Halleck's argument that without the physical senses to provide data, the mind—no matter how perfect its potential—remains entirely empty and inactive. It then introduces the idea that physical expressions and emotions are so intertwined that their causal relationship is difficult to determine.</summary>

<keywords>sensory deprivation, perception, nervous system, memory, Professor Halleck, William James, psychology of emotion, physiological expression</keywords>
