<meta>This page continues the introduction to the theories of William James, specifically his argument that physical reactions precede the experience of emotion.</meta>

<page-num>208</page-num>

<header>Mind and Body</header>

James, in some of his works, strongly indicates this close relationship, for instance when he says: “The feeling, in the more basic <note>original: "coarser"</note> emotions, results from the bodily expression. . . . My theory is that the bodily changes follow directly the perception of the exciting fact, and that our feeling of these same changes as they occur is the emotion. . . . Particular perceptions certainly do produce widespread bodily effects by a sort of immediate physical influence, occurring before <note>original: "antecedent to"</note> the arousal of an emotion or emotional idea. . . . Every one of the bodily changes, whatever it may be, is *felt*, acutely or vaguely <note>original: "obscurely"</note>, the moment it occurs. . . . If we imagine <note>original: "fancy"</note> some strong emotion, and then try to remove <note>original: "abstract"</note> from our consciousness of it all the feelings of its bodily symptoms, we have nothing left behind. . . . A disembodied human emotion is a complete non-existence <note>original: "nonentity"</note>. I do not say that it is a contradiction in the nature of things, or that pure spirits are necessarily condemned to cold intellectual lives; but I say that for *us* emotion dissociated from all bodily feelings is inconceivable. The more closely I examine <note>original: "scrutinize"</note> my states, the

<summary>William James argues that emotions are fundamentally the brain's perception of physical changes in the body, suggesting that without bodily sensations, emotion itself cannot exist.</summary>

<keywords>William James, James-Lange theory, emotion, physical expression, perception, bodily changes, psychology</keywords>

--- Page 215 ---
<meta>This page continues the quote from William James regarding his "sensational theory" of emotion and transitions into the author's summary of the interdependence of the mental and physical states.</meta>

<page-num>209</page-num>

<header>REACTION OF THE PHYSICAL</header>

> ...the more persuaded I become that whatever "basic" <note>original: "coarse"</note> feelings <note>original: "affections"</note> and passions I have are, in truth, made up of those bodily changes which we usually call their expression or result. . . . But our emotions must always be, internally, what they are, regardless of the physiological basis for their appearance <note>original: "apparition"</note>. If they are deep, pure, worthy, and spiritual facts under any conceivable theory of their physiological source, they remain just as deep, spiritual, and worthy of respect under this current theory of sensation <note>original: "sensational theory," referring to the idea that emotions are the perception of physical sensations</note>."

A deeper look at the relationship between Mind and Body would require us to enter the field of metaphysical speculation, which I have already said I want to avoid. For our current purposes, it is enough to: *recognize that every person has both a mind and a physical body; that these two parts or aspects of the self are closely linked by an endless variety of connections; that these two parts of our being constantly and continuously influence <note>original: "act and react upon"</note> each other; and that in all considerations of the ques-*

<summary>William James concludes that emotions remain significant and spiritual even if they are rooted in physical sensations, while the author emphasizes the constant, reciprocal influence between the mind and the physical body.</summary>

<keywords>William James, sensational theory, physiology, mind-body connection, metaphysics, emotions, physical expression</keywords>

--- Page 216 ---
<meta>This page concludes the sentence from the previous page regarding the consideration of well-being, finishing Atkinson's argument for a holistic view of health that unites the study of the mind and the body.</meta>

<page-num>210</page-num>
<header>MIND AND BODY</header>

*...tion of either mental or physical well-being, or both, both of these aspects of being must be considered; any system of medical treatment <note>original: "therapeutics," the branch of medicine concerned with the treatment of disease</note> which ignores either of these aspects is necessarily "one-sided" and incomplete. While we may consider the Mind and the Body as separate and independent for the sake of clear, specialized thinking, we must, in the end, recognize their interdependence, mutual relationship, and how they act and react upon one another.*

Thus, the "New Psychology" <note>Atkinson refers to the then-emerging field of experimental psychology which began to account for biological factors</note> recognizes the importance of the body, while the "New Physiology" recognizes the importance of the mind. In the end, we feel that both physiology and psychology must be recognized as being merely two different phases of one great science—the Science of Life.

->THE END<- <note>original: "FINIS"</note>
